{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}V_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\otimes\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}

Then
\mathcal{D}_{(V_{0},b_{0})}
is a deformation category over
(\mathrm{Art}/\Lambda)
, where
b_{0}:\hat{U}\to f^{*}V_{0}
is induced by the chosen identification. The family
(\Lambda,\hat{U})
yields a morphism
\Spec\Lambda\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
, and
\mathcal{D}_{(V_{0},b_{0})}
is easily seen to be the deformation category associated to the fiber 2-product
\Spec\Lambda\times_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)}\mathcal{M}_{B}(Y)
at the point
(V_{0},b_{0})
. In particular, since
f^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{B}(Y)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
is representable,
\mathcal{D}_{(V_{0},b_{0})}
will be equivalent to
\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}
for a complete
\Lambda
-algebra
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
. If
(\Lambda,\hat{U})
is miniversal, the morphism
\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(Y)
will only be versal in general.

The relative deformation theory will be governed by the natural exact triangle

\mathbb{C}_{Y}\to Rf_{*}\mathbb{C}_{X}\to M_{X/Y}\to\mathbb{C}_{Y}[1].

As before, the splitting of
f^{*}N
gives a morphism
N\to Rf_{*}f^{*}(J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0})[1]
and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0}[2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}f^{*}(J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0})[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}M_{X/Y}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0}[2].}

Lemma 4.48
.

(1)

The resulting element of
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(V_{0},J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}M_{X/Y}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0}[1])
is uniquely determined and vanishes if and only if there is a relative lift
V^{\prime}\to V
,
\hat{U}\to f^{*}V^{\prime}
.

(2)

The group
J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(V_{0},M_{X/Y}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0})
acts simply transitively on the set of isomorphism classes of such lifts
\mathcal{D}_{(V,b)}(A^{\prime})
, provided it is nonempty.

(3)

The relative tangent space is
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(V_{0},M_{X/Y}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0})
.

(4)

There are no first order automorphisms.

Proof.

For (1), the dashed arrow is uniquely determined up to
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0}[2],Rf_{*}f^{*}(J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0})[1])=0
, and if it vanishes there is a splitting
N\to J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0}[1]
compatible with the splitting of
f^{*}N
. Part (4) is clear from the definition. Since
J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(V_{0},V_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}M_{X/Y})
acts transitively on the splittings of
N
compatible with that of
f^{*}N
, this together with (4) yields (2) and therefore (3).
∎

4.8.2.
Framing

Note that for
X=\mathrm{pt}
we have
\mathcal{M}_{B}(\mathrm{pt},r)=[\mathbf{GL}_{r}\backslash\mathrm{pt}]
and the miniversal family is the quotient morphism
\mathrm{pt}=R_{B}(\mathrm{pt},\mathrm{pt},r)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(\mathrm{pt},r)
. For general
X
,
R_{B}(X,x)
is the fibered 2-product of
i^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(x)
and
R_{B}(x,x)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(x)
where
i:\{x\}\to X
is the inclusion, so the deformation theory of
R_{B}(X,x)
can be thought of as the relative deformation theory of
i^{*}
.

4.8.3.
The diagonal

Let
\Lambda
be a complete noetherian local ring,
U_{1},U_{2}
two free
\Lambda
-local systems, and
f_{0}:U_{1,0}\to U_{2,0}
an isomorphism over the closed points,
U_{i,0}:=\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{i}
. The deformation category
\mathcal{D}_{f_{0}}
of the fibered 2-product of the resulting morphisms
\Spec\Lambda\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
at the point
(U_{1,0},U_{2,0},f_{0})
is the category cofibered in groupoids over
(\mathrm{Art}/\Lambda)
consisting of pairs
(A,f)
where
A
is a
\Lambda
-algebra and
f:A\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{1}\to A\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{2}
is an isomorphism of
A
-local systems, with the obvious notion of isomorphism.

Let
J\to A^{\prime}\to A
be a small extension in
(\mathrm{Art}/\Lambda)
and
(A,f)
an object of
\mathcal{D}_{f_{0}}
. We have a short exact sequence of
A^{\prime}
-local systems

\displaystyle 0\to\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(U_{1,0},J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}U_{2,0})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}_{A^{\prime}}(A^{\prime}\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{1},A^{\prime}\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{2})

(4.8.5)

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}_{A}(A\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{1},A\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{2})\to 0.

Taking cohomology, there is an exact sequence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to\Hom_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(U_{1,0},J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}U_{2,0})\to\Hom_{A^{\prime}_{X}}(A^{\prime}\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{1},A^{\prime}\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{2})\to\Hom_{A_{X}}(A\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{1},A\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{2})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\partial}\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(U_{1,0},J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}U_{2,0})

Lemma 4.49
.

(1)

The element
\partial f\in J\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(U_{1,0},J\otimes U_{2,0})
is an obstruction for lifting
(A,f)
.

(2)

The tangent space of
\mathcal{D}_{f_{0}}
is naturally identified with
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(U_{1,0},U_{2,0})
.

(3)

There are no infinitesimal automorphisms.

4.8.4.
Fixed local monodromy

For simplicity, let
(\bar{X},D)
be a log smooth projective curve with
X=\bar{X}\setminus D
and
j:X\to\bar{X}
the inclusion. Note that in this case, for a complex local system
W
on
X
, the intermediate extension
j_{!*}(W[1])
is just
(j_{*}W)[1]
, and that
Rj_{*}
is perverse-exact. For each
\bar{x}\in D
take a small embedded punctured disk
j_{\bar{x}}:\mathbb{D}^{*}\to\bar{X}
whose interior closure is a small disk neighborhood of
\bar{x}
and consider the representable morphism of algebraic stacks

(4.8.6)

\psi_{D}:=\prod_{\bar{x}\in D}j_{\bar{x}}^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)\to\prod_{\bar{x}\in D}\mathcal{M}_{B}(\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{*}).

where by
\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
we mean the germ of
\mathbb{D}^{*}
around the origin. For
V_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)(\mathbb{C})
we define the fiber of this morphism over the image of
V_{0}
, namely
FM(V_{0}):=(\psi_{D})^{-1}(\psi_{D}V_{0})
, to be the fixed local monodromy leaf at
V_{0}
. Here by
j^{*}_{D^{\mathrm{loc}}}V_{0}
we mean the locally closed substack supported at
V_{0}
. As the terminology suggests, this is the locally closed
6
6

6

Note: For every
X
, every
\mathbb{C}
-point of
\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
is locally closed: every orbit
O
in
R_{B}(X,x)
is constructible and irreducible, hence contains a point
\rho
which is in the interior of the closure
\bar{O}
, and so the same is true for every point of
O
.
substack of
\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
of local systems with the same local monodromy as
V_{0}
up to conjugation (separately for each point of
D
). It does do not depend on the choice of the cover.

We now consider the relative deformation theory of the embedding
FM(V_{0})\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
. Fix a miniversal local system
(\Lambda,\hat{V})
at
V_{0}
and let
\mathcal{D}
be the full subcategory of
(\mathrm{Art}/\Lambda)
of
A
for which
A\otimes_{\Lambda}\hat{V}
has fixed local monodromy. Let
J\to A^{\prime}\to A
be a small extension of local artinian
\Lambda
-algebras and suppose
V:=A\otimes_{\Lambda}\hat{V}
has fixed local monodromy.
Set
V^{\prime}=A^{\prime}\otimes_{\Lambda}\hat{V}
. According to
Remark

4.44
, the obstruction at each point
\bar{x}\in D
is naturally an element of the stalk
J\otimes(R^{1}j_{*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0}))_{\bar{x}}
, but we want to interpret this obstruction in terms of functors that lift to Hodge/twistor modules. Note first that we can interpret this group as
J\otimes H^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0}))
, where
\psi_{\bar{x}}:=\psi_{q_{\bar{x}}}
is the nearby cycles functor for a choice of coordinate
q_{\bar{x}}
at
\bar{x}
equipped with its natural
\mathbb{C}[T,T^{-1}]
-module structure given by the local monodromy operator.

Since
\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}
is exact on local systems, we have a short exact sequence

0\to\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})\to\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},\mathfrak{m}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes_{A}V)\to\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},\mathfrak{m}_{A}\otimes_{A}V)\to 0

of
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}]=\mathbb{C}[T,T^{-1}]
-modules, and because
V
has fixed local monodromy, this sequence is exact after applying
H^{i}(\mathbb{Z},-)
. Thus there is a natural short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0}))

\displaystyle\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},\mathfrak{m}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes_{A}V))

\displaystyle\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},\mathfrak{m}_{A}\otimes_{A}V))\to 0

Consider
E^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V^{\prime})
the sub
A^{\prime}
-local system of
\mathbb{C}
-linear morphisms for which the composition
V_{0}\to V^{\prime}\to V_{0}
is a multiple of the identity. Define
E
similarly for
V
. Then we have a commutative diagram with exact rows and columns:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\otimes\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\otimes\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},\mathfrak{m}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes_{A}V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},\mathfrak{m}_{A}\otimes_{A}V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

After applying
\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}
, the bottom row splits as an extension of
\mathbb{C}[T,T^{-1}]
-modules, so the extension class of the middle row yields a class
\mathrm{obs}_{A/A^{\prime}}\in J\otimes H^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0}))
. This class vanishes if and only if
V^{\prime}
has fixed local monodromy, so we have proven:

Lemma 4.50
.

(1)

There are no relative automorphisms or deformations for
FM(V_{0})\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
.

(2)

The class
\mathrm{obs}_{A/A^{\prime}}\in J\otimes H^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0}))
is a relative obstruction for
FM(V_{0})\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
.

Proof.

The first part is clear since
FM(V_{0})\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
is a substack, and the second part is the above argument.
∎

We can interpret the obstruction naturally in terms of the intersection cohomology
IH^{1}(\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})):=H^{0}\mathrm{pt}_{*}j_{!*}(\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})[1])
using the canonical short exact sequence of perverse sheaves

0\to j_{!*}W[1]\to Rj_{*}W[1]\to i_{*}i^{!}j_{!*}(W[1])[1]\to 0.

Lemma 4.51
.

Let
i_{\bar{x}}:\bar{x}\to\bar{X}
be the inclusion. For any local system
W
on
X
the natural morphism of functors
\psi_{\bar{x}}\to i_{\bar{x}}^{!}
induces an isomorphism
H^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}W[1])\to i_{\bar{x}}^{!}(j_{!*}W[1])[1]
. In particular, there is a natural exact exact sequence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to IH^{1}(X,\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0}))\to H^{1}(X,\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0}))\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\psi_{\bar{x}}j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(V_{0},V_{0}))

\displaystyle\to IH^{2}(X,\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0}))\to 0.

Moreover, if
V_{0}
underlies a pure
\mathbb{C}
-VMS, then
j_{!*}(\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})[1])=W_{1}(\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})[1])
is pure, as is
IH^{1}(X,\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0}))=W_{1}H^{1}(X,\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0}))
.

Proof.

The first part is standard from the description of perverse sheaves on the disk. For the second part, the mixed module
\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})[1]
has pure weight one, as does
j_{!*}(\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})[1])
, so
i^{!}j_{!*}(\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})[1])[1]
has weights
\geq 2
, and proper push-forward preserves weights.
∎

where the first morphism is the universal extension. This composition is equal to the composition

where the first map is the one associated to
\mathfrak{m}_{A}\to\mathfrak{m}_{A}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{2}=\mathfrak{t}_{A}^{\vee}
. The obstruction
\mathrm{obs}^{\mathrm{MS}}_{A^{\prime}/A}(V)
can be thought of as the composition of

and
\mathfrak{m}_{A}\otimes_{A}V\to J\otimes_{\mathbf{T}(0)}V_{0}[1]
. A map

acts on the extension

and modifies the corresponding map

by adding the composition
\mathfrak{m}_{A}\otimes_{A}V\to\mathfrak{t}_{A}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbf{T}(0)}V_{0}\to J\otimes_{\mathbf{T}(0)}V_{0}[1]
while fixing

. The obstruction element is then modified by adding the composition

, so by the above (c) is proven.

The remaining conditions (7)-(9) are easily verified using

and (

) as in

.
∎

4.9.1.
The diagonal

We continue the discussion from

4.8.3
.

Let
\Lambda
be a pro-
\mathbf{T}(0)
-MS-algebra,

\Lambda
-AVMS, and

-AVMS. We let

Proposition 4.54
.

\mathcal{D}_{f_{0}}^{\mathrm{MS}}\to(\mathrm{MS}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{Art}/\Lambda)
is part of a precise MS-enhancement of
\mathcal{D}_{|f_{0}|}\to(\mathrm{Art}/|\Lambda|)
.

Proof.

We take
M=\mathrm{pt}_{X*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(U_{1,0},U_{2,0})[-1]
and set
\mathfrak{t}=\mathscr{H}^{1}(M)=\Hom_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(U_{1,0},U_{2,0})
,
\mathfrak{o}=\mathscr{H}^{2}(M)=\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(U_{1,0},U_{2,0})
. The first order element is given by the universal homomorphism

\mathbf{T}(0)[\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}]\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{1}\to\mathbf{T}(0)[\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}]\otimes_{\Lambda}U_{2}

where
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
is square zero and
\mathbf{T}(0)[\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}]
has the trivial
\Lambda
-algebra structure.

Suppose

is a small extension in
(\mathrm{MS}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{Art}/\Lambda)
and
(A,f)
an object of
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{MS}}_{f_{0}}
. Then (
4.8.5
) is a short exact sequence of
A^{\prime}
-AVMS, and as in (
4.49
) the element
\partial f\in\Hom_{\operatorname{MM}}(U_{1,0},J\otimes_{\mathbf{T}(0)}U_{2,0}[1])
obstructs the existence of a lift. Moreover, twisting the mixed structure on
A^{\prime}
by an element of
\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MS}}(\mathbf{T}(0),J\otimes_{\mathbf{T}(0)}\mathfrak{t}_{A/\Lambda})
will act in the required way on the obstruction class. Finally, the set of lifts is clearly a torsor over
\Hom_{\mathrm{MS}}(\mathbf{T}(0),J\otimes_{\mathbf{T}(0)}\mathfrak{t})
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.3
.

Parts (1) and (2) follow from
Proposition

4.52
and
Proposition

4.43
, while
Proposition

4.54
and
Proposition

4.43
give part (3). The last statement follows since if
X
is normal and
V_{0}
underlies a
\mathbb{C}
-VHS then the Hodge structure on
\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(V_{0},V_{0})
has weights
\geq 1
, for instance by the computation in
Lemma

4.51
(after reducing to a curve by Lefschetz).
∎

4.9.3.
Fixed local monodromy

We continue the discussion of
Section

4.8.4
.

If
V_{0}
underlies a
\mathbb{C}
-AVMS, then we may equip
\Lambda
(resp.
\hat{V}
) with a pro-
\mathbf{T}(0)
-
\mathrm{MS}
-algebra (resp. pro-
\Lambda
-AVMS) structure by
Theorem

4.3
. Let
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{MS}}
be the full subcategory of
(\mathrm{MS}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{Art}/\Lambda)
(considered as a category cofibered in groupoids over
(\mathrm{MS}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{Art}/\Lambda)
) whose objects are
A
for which
A\otimes_{\Lambda}\hat{V}
has fixed local monodromy as a local system at each point of
D
.

The following proposition demonstrates that showing a full sub-deformation category of a deformation category admitting an enhancement itself admits an enhancement is much easier—we must just verify the relative obstructions are Tate classes in a mixed structure.

Proposition 4.56
.

\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{MS}}\to(\mathrm{MS}\textrm{-}\mathrm{Art}/\Lambda)^{\mathrm{op}}
is part of a precise Hodge enhancement of
\mathcal{D}\to(\mathrm{Art}/|\Lambda|)^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Proof.

As there are no infinitesimal automorphisms or deformations, we need only show the relative obstruction class for a small extension
J\to A^{\prime}\to A
in
(\mathrm{MS}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{Art}/\Lambda)
for which
A\otimes_{\Lambda}U
has fixed local monodromy is a Tate class of
J\otimes i^{!}(j_{!*}\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.99997pt\operatorname{om}(V_{0},V_{0})[1])
, which is clear from the construction leading up to
Lemma

4.50
.
∎

4.9.4.
Fixed residual eigenvalues

Continuing the setup of
Section

4.8.4
, consider instead the map to the good moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{B}(X,r)\to\prod_{\bar{x}\in D}M_{B}(\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{*},r)\cong\prod_{\bar{x}\in D}\Sym^{r}\mathbf{G}_{m}

where the isomorphism is by taking the characteristic polynomial. We call the fiber through
V_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)(\mathbb{C})
the fixed residual eigenvalues leaf
FE(V_{0})
. If
V_{0}
underlies a
\mathbb{C}
-AVMS and we again equip
\Lambda
(resp.
\hat{V}
) with a pro-
\mathbf{T}(0)
-
\mathrm{MS}
-algebra (resp. pro-
\Lambda
-AVMS) structure by
Theorem

4.3
, then for each
\bar{x}\in D
,
\psi_{\bar{x}}\hat{V}
is a pro-
\Lambda
-MS-module, and comes equipped with an endomorphism
T_{\bar{x}}
compatible with this structure. Then the ideal of
FE(V_{0})
is cut out by the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial
p_{T_{\bar{x}}}
of
T_{\bar{x}}
for each
\bar{x}\in D
, which is naturally a pro-MS-ideal.

4.10.
Hodge/twistor substacks of
\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)

Observe for the following definition that for a morphism of algebraic stacks
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
and miniversal points
\hat{m}:\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M},m}\to\mathcal{M}
,
\hat{n}:\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{N},n}\to\mathcal{N}
at closed points
m\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
,
n:=f(m)
, respectively, the composition
\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M},m}\to\mathcal{N}
lifts to
\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M},m}\to\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{N},n}
and so there is a pullback map
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{N},n}\to\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M},m}
which is noncanonical but determined up to first order.

We have the following compatibilities:

Lemma 4.57
.

With the above notation, assume we have a closed immersions
g:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{N}
and
z\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{C})
with
g(z)\cong n
(with a choice of isomorphism). Then:

(1)

\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{Z},z}:=\mathcal{Z}\times_{\mathcal{N}}\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{N},n}\to\mathcal{Z}
is miniversal at
z
.

(2)

\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}:=\Spec(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{Z},z}\otimes_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{N},n}}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M},m})\to\mathcal{Z}\times_{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{M}
is miniversal at
(z,m)
.

Proof.

For any group
G
and any
G
-representation
V
, the only closed substacks of
[G\backslash\Spec(\mathbb{C}\oplus V)]
are of the form
[G\backslash\Spec(\mathbb{C}\oplus U)]
for a subrepresentation
U\subset V
. This implies that the base-change of the first order neighborhood
[G\backslash\Spec(\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{\mathcal{N},n}^{\vee})]
of
n
is the first order neighborhood
[G\backslash\Spec(\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{\mathcal{Z},z}^{\vee})]
of
z
, where
G
is the inertia of
n
(and thus
z
). Since the base-change of
\hat{n}
is formally smooth, this gives (1).

For (2), we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M},m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}\times_{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{Z},z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{N},n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}}

with Cartesian front, bottom, and back faces. It follows that the top face is Cartesian, so by (1) the conclusion follows.
∎

In the following definition we only consider the formal twistor geometry at semisimple points with quasiunipotent local monodromy: the former condition ensures there is a canonical choice of variation of twistor structures, and the latter, while not necessary here, allows for a simpler construction of the Deligne–Hitchin space in
Section

8
and will be sufficient for our purposes.

Definition 4.58
.

Let
X
be a connected algebraic space and
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
a locally closed algebraic substack.

(1)

We say
\mathcal{Z}
is
formally Hodge
at a
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-fixed point
V\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{ss}}
(see
Section

7.5
) if, equipping
V
with its canonical weight zero
\mathbb{C}
-VHS
8
8

8

By Deligne
[
33
, Proposition 1.13]
, for any
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-fixed semisimple complex local system
V
, there is a unique weight zero
\mathbb{C}
-VHS structure on
V
for which any simple sub-local system is a sub-
\mathbb{C}
-VHS.
, there is a surjective morphism of pro-
\mathbb{C}(0)
-MHS-algebras
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X),V}\to\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{Z},V}
for which
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{Z},V}
is miniversal for
\mathcal{Z}
at
V
.

(2)

We say
\mathcal{Z}
is a
formally twistor at a point

V\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{ss}}
if, equipping
V
with its canonical (tame purely imaginary) variation of twistor structures (as in

4.19

\mathbf{T}(0)
-MTS-algebras
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X),V}\to\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{Z},V}
for which
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{Z},V}
is miniversal for
\mathcal{Z}
at
V
.

(3)

The substack
\mathcal{Z}
is
formally twistor
if
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{C})
is closed under semisimplification, formally twistor at every point of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{ss}}
, and formally Hodge at every point underlying a
\mathbb{C}-\mathrm{VHS}
with quasiunipotent local monodromy.

We likewise define formally twistor subvarieties of
R_{B}(X,x)
.

Note that by
Lemma

4.6
any closed subset of
\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)(\mathbb{C})
is closed under semisimplification.

Lemma 4.59
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
be a locally closed algebraic substack,
V\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{C})
,
\Spec\Lambda\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
a versal family at
V
underlying a
\Lambda
-AVMS, and
\Spec\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}:=\mathcal{Z}\times_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)}\Spec\Lambda\to\Spec\Lambda
the base-change. Then
\mathcal{Z}
is formally Hodge (resp. twistor) at
V
if and only if
\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}
is a (surjective) morphism of pro-
\mathbb{C}(0)
-MHS-algebras (resp. pro-
\mathbf{T}(0)
-MTS-algebras).

Proof.

Let
\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X),V}\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
be the miniversal family at
V
, and consider the completion of the fibered 2-product at an identification of the closed points of
\Spec\Lambda
and
\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X),V}
in the solid diagram below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\mathcal{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X),V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B}(X).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}

Let the diagonal arrows be the base-change of
\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
. By
Theorem

4.3
(3), the solid left and solid top arrows correspond to morphisms of pro-
\mathbf{T}(0)
-MS-algebras. The diagonal morphisms are surjective on algebras, and the horizontal and vertical ones are injective on algebras (as they are formally smooth), except those involving
\mathcal{Z}
. Thus, if
\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is a morphism of pro-
\mathbf{T}(0)
-MS-algebras, then so are the composition
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)}\to\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}
and its corestriction to its image
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X),V}\to\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{Z}}
. The converse is clear since
\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is the tensor product of
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X),V}\to\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{Z}}
by
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X),V}\to\mathcal{S}
. By the same argument,
\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is a morphism of pro-
\mathbf{T}(0)
-MS-algebras if and only if
\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}
is.
∎

Corollary 4.60
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
be a locally closed algebraic substack and
R\mathcal{Z}\subset R_{B}(X,x)
the base-change by the quotient map. Then
\mathcal{Z}
is formally twistor if and only if
R\mathcal{Z}
is.

Proposition 4.61
.

Let
X
be connected normal algebraic space.

(1)

For any
r

\mathcal{M}_{B}(X,r)
and
\{\mathrm{triv}_{r}\}
are formally twistor substacks of
\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
.

(2)

Assume

. For any

the fixed residual eigenvalues leaf
FE(V)\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
is a closed formally twistor substack.

(3)

Intersections and reductions of formally twistor substacks are formally twistor substacks. Irreducible components
9
9

9

These operations are defined by taking the quotients of the corresponding operations on the base-change to the framed moduli space—see
Section

5.1
.
of reduced twistor substacks are formally twistor substacks. Finite unions of closed twistor substacks are twistor substacks.

(4)

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of connected normal algebraic spaces and
f^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{B}(Y)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
the pullback morphism. Then:

(a)

For any formally twistor substack
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
,
(f^{*})^{-1}(\mathcal{Z})\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(Y)
is a formally twistor substack.

(b)

For any formally twistor substack
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(Y)
,

is a formally twistor substack provided

is dominant or a Lefschetz curve (see

4.8
).

(5)

Inverse images of formally twistor substacks under the direct sum and tensor product morphisms
\oplus,\otimes:\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)^{2}\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
are formally twistor substacks
10
10

10

With the obvious definition of formally twistor substack of
\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)^{2}
.
.

Proof.

The first part of (

) is clear and the second follows from (

) via pullback from a point. In parts (2) and (3), the closure under semisimplification follows since the semisimplification of a point is contained in the closure of that point, by

. Part (

4.56
and the discussion in
Section

4.9.4
. The reduction and irreducible component part of (

) follows from

4.36
, since by
Theorem

4.3
and the normality of

the maximal ideal of the twistor algebras are the weight -1 part.

For the remaining parts of (

)-(

), all of the operations are compatible with the quotient map from the framed moduli space, so by
Corollary

4.60
it suffices to prove the corresponding statements in the framed case. The intersection part of (

) is clear. The union part follows since the completed local ring of the union is the image of the completed local ring of the product.

For part (4b), under the hypotheses on
f
,
f^{*}
is immersive
11
11

11

By immersive we mean a locally closed immersion locally on the source.
by
Lemma

4.62
below. The closure under semisimplification condition in parts (

) and (

) follows from the fact that semisimplification commutes with pullback, direct sum, and tensor product—in the pullback case we must use
Theorem

. This in particular implies that semisimple points map to semisimple points and semisimple points in an image lift to semisimple points under these functors. Moreover, the pullback of a local system with quasiunipotent local monodromy has quasiunipotent local monodromy, and in part (4b) under the assumptions on
f
the converse also holds by
Remark

4.10
. Altogether, this implies that in part (4a) we have
((f^{*})^{-1}\mathcal{Z})(\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{ss}}\subset(f^{*})^{-1}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{ss}})
and in part (4b) we have
(f^{*}\mathcal{Z})(\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{ss}}=f^{*}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{qu},\mathrm{ss}})
. Finally, we must use the same property for
\mathbb{C}-\mathrm{VHS}
(which follows from the
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-action—see
Section

7.5
).

Part (
4a
) is then immediate from

4.57
(2) and

4.2
(3). Part (

) follows from

(3) as well since the completed local ring of the image is the image of the pullback map on completed local rings. For part (
5
), we consider the fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{B}(X,x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{B}(X,x)^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B}(X).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus\text{,}\otimes}

Since sums and tensor products of AVMS are AVMS, by

∎

Lemma 4.62
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism of algebraic spaces.

(1)

If
f_{*}\colon\pi(X,x)\to\pi_{1}(Y,y)
is surjective, then
f^{*}\colon\mathcal{M}_{B}(Y)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
is a closed immersion.

(2)

If
f
is dominant and
Y
is normal, then
f^{*}\colon\mathcal{M}_{B}(Y)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)
is immersive.

Proof.

The first part is clear on representation spaces. For the second part, there is a locally closed subspace
i\colon Z\to X
such that
f|_{Z}\colon Z\to Y
is generically finite, and if
f|_{Z}^{*}=i^{*}\circ f^{*}
is immersive, then so is
f^{*}
.
Thus we reduce to
f
generically finite. There is a dense Zariski open
U\subset Y
for which the base-change
f_{U}\colon X_{U}\to U
is finite étale, and since by normality and part (1) the restrictions
\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X_{U})
and
\mathcal{M}_{B}(Y)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(U)
are closed immersions, we reduce to the case
f
is finite étale. In this case,
\mathbb{C}_{Y}\to f_{*}\mathbb{C}_{X}
splits, so the map on tangent spaces
\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{Y}}(V,V)\to\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{C}_{X}}(f^{*}V,f^{*}V)
is split as well, hence injective.
∎

5.
Bialgebraic sets of local systems and density of the quasiunipotent locus

In this section we define constructible, bialgebraic, and
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-bialgebraic sets of local systems, and prove
Theorem

1.11
. Once the basic definitions are made, the result will follow rather easily from known transcendence theorems (namely Ax–Lindemann for the exponential map and the Gelfond–Schneider theorem). The main subtlety is that for
X
non-proper, some care must be taken on the De Rham side, as the naive De Rham analytic space does not have an algebraic structure. This is essentially because the condition that the singularities be regular implicitly makes reference to the existence of an extension as a connection with log poles to a log smooth compactification, and there is a non-finite-type choice of such an extension.

Much of the general formalism of constructible subsets of stacks and bialgebraic subsets of the Betti stack have been treated elsewhere (Simpson
[
106
]
in the projective case, and Budur, Wang, and Lerer
[
23
,
22
]
in the quasiprojective case) in roughly equivalent ways. We direct the reader to the corresponding sections along the way.

5.1.
Constructible subsets of algebraic stacks

For the remainder, unless otherwise indicated,
X
will be a connected smooth algebraic space defined over an algebraically closed subfield
k\subset\mathbb{C}
. We use the embedding to define the analytification
X^{\mathrm{an}}
. We say a subset
\Sigma\subset X(\mathbb{C})
has nonempty interior in each component of
X
if, for each irreducible component
X_{0}
of
X
, the intersection
\Sigma\cap X_{0}
has nonempty interior in
X_{0}
with respect to the euclidean topology.

Given an algebraic stack
\mathcal{M}
over
\mathbb{C}
, it will be useful to have a reasonable notion of constructible subsets of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
. We have the following easy lemma:

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
f:V\to U
be a smooth morphism of finite type complex algebraic spaces which is surjective on points. Then
\Sigma\subset U(\mathbb{C})
is Zariski (open/closed/constructible), (open/closed) in the euclidean topology, or analytically constructible
12
12

12

That is, in the Boolean algebra generated by closed analytic subvarieties.
if and only if
f^{-1}(\Sigma)
is.

Proof.

All the claims follow from the existence of sections locally on
U
(using surjectivity) in the euclidean and étale topologies and Chevalley’s theorem.
∎

For some smooth atlas
\pi:U\to\mathcal{M}
by an algebraic space, we may equip
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
with the quotient Zariski or euclidean topology via the surjective map
\pi:U(\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
, which by the lemma is independent of the atlas. We say a subset
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
is (Zariski/analytically) constructible
13
13

13

We often just say “constructible” if we mean Zariski constructible if there’s no chance of confusion.
if
\pi^{-1}(\Sigma)
is, which by the lemma is again independent of the atlas, and we may likewise define both the Zariski closure
\Sigma^{\mathrm{Zar}}
and the euclidean closure
\bar{\Sigma}
. Note that
\pi^{-1}(\Sigma)^{\mathrm{Zar}}=\pi^{-1}(\Sigma^{\mathrm{Zar}})
and
\overline{\pi^{-1}(\Sigma)}=\pi^{-1}\left(\overline{\Sigma}\right)
for any atlas. We say
\Sigma
is irreducible if it is irreducible with respect to the Zariski topology, meaning every nonempty open subset of
\Sigma
is dense. This is equivalent to there being a morphism
f:Z\to\mathcal{M}
from an irreducible algebraic space
Z
whose image is contained and dense in
\Sigma^{\mathrm{Zar}}
, since on the one hand images of irreducible topological spaces are irreducible, and on the other hand any irreducible component of the preimage of
\Sigma
in an atlas has image in
\Sigma
with nonempty interior. Finally, observe that for any Zariski closed
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{M}
, there is a unique closed immersion of algebraic stacks
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathcal{M}
with
\Sigma=\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
. Indeed, given a presentation in groupoids
R\rightrightarrows U\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathcal{M}
, we take the closed subspace
V=\pi^{-1}(\Sigma)
and the induced full presentation in groupoids
R_{V}\rightrightarrows V
where
R_{V}=R\times_{U\times U}V\times V
.

Remark 5.2
.

In the case of a global quotient
\mathcal{M}=[\mathbf{G}\backslash X]
of an algebraic space by an algebraic group, the Zariski/euclidean topology on
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
is the quotient topology, and the algebra of constructible subsets is equivalent to the algebra of invariant constructible subsets of
X
.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an algebraic stack over
\mathbb{C}
. The algebra of constructible subsets of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
is the Boolean algebra generated by finite type Zariski closed subsets.

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-morphism of algebraic stacks over
\mathbb{C}
. For any constructible subset
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
(resp. constructible subset
\mathrm{T}\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
), the image
f(\Sigma)\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
(resp. preimage
f^{-1}(\mathrm{T})\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
) is constructible.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-morphism between two finite type algebraic stacks over
\mathbb{C}
.

(1)

if
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
has nonempty interior in each component of its Zariski closure, then
f(\Sigma)\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
has nonempty interior in each component in its Zariski closure.

(2)

Assume that
f
is universally open (e.g. flat). If
\mathrm{T}\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
has nonempty interior in each component in its Zariski closure, then
f^{-1}(\mathrm{T})\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
has nonempty interior in each component in its Zariski closure.

Proof.

One reduces immediately to the case where both
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
are algebraic spaces of finite type. We start with
(1)
. Let
Z
be an irreducible component of the Zariski closure
\Sigma^{\mathrm{Zar}}
of
\Sigma
in
\mathcal{M}
. Then
\Sigma\cap Z(\mathbb{C})
is Zariski dense in
Z
and has nonempty interior in
Z(\mathbb{C})
with respect to the euclidean topology. Therefore, one is reduced to consider the case where both
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
are irreducible algebraic spaces and
f
is dominant. In particular, every Zariski closed strict subset of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
or
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
has empty interior with respect to the euclidean topology. Let
U\subset\mathcal{N}
be a dense Zariski-open subset such that
\mathcal{M}_{U}\to U
is flat. The induced holomorphic map
\mathcal{M}_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}\to U^{\mathrm{an}}
is therefore flat too, hence in particular open. The set
\Sigma\cap\mathcal{M}_{U}(\mathbb{C})
has nonempty interior in
\mathcal{M}_{U}(\mathbb{C})
, hence its image
f(\Sigma\cap\mathcal{M}_{U}(\mathbb{C}))=f(\Sigma)\cap U(\mathbb{C})
has nonempty interior in
U^{\mathrm{an}}
. A fortiori,
f(\Sigma)
has non-empty interior in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{an}}
. This shows
(1)
.

Let us now prove
(2)
. Base-changing
f
along the open inclusion
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{Zar}}\subset\mathcal{N}
, one can assume that
\mathrm{T}
is Zariski-dense in
\mathcal{N}
. Moreover, working component by component, it is sufficient to treat the case where
\mathcal{N}
is irreducible. Since
f
is universally open, one has
f^{-1}(\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{Zar}})=f^{-1}(\mathrm{T})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
. Let
Z\subset\mathcal{M}
be an irreducible component of
f^{-1}(\mathrm{T})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
. Since
f
is open, its image
f(Z)
is a dense constructible subset of
\mathcal{N}
, hence it contains a non-empty Zariski open subset
U\subset\mathcal{N}
. Let
V
be a nonempty euclidean open subset of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{an}}
contained in
\Sigma
. Since
U^{\mathrm{an}}
is dense in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{an}}
, the intersection
U^{\mathrm{an}}\cap V
is nonempty. Therefore, by shrinking
V
, one can assume that
V\subset U^{\mathrm{an}}
. It follows that
Z^{\mathrm{an}}
contains the nonempty open subset
f^{-1}(V)
. Since
f^{-1}(V)\subset f^{-1}(\mathrm{T})
, this completes the proof. ∎

If
\mathcal{M}
is defined over
k\subset\mathbb{C}
we likewise define the notions of
k
-constructible subsets of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})
and their
k
-Zariski closures by using a smooth atlas defined over
k
. For any
k
-morphism
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
of
k
-algebraic stacks, there is an induced map
f(\mathbb{C}):\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{C})
under which images and preimages of
k
-constructible subsets are
k
-constructible.

5.2.
Correspondence stacks and morphisms

By a correspondence stack
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathcal{Z})
we mean a pair of algebraic stacks
\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B}
together with an analytic stack
\mathcal{Z}
and a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\mathrm{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\mathrm{an}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{DR}}

If
\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B}
are algebraic spaces (in which case we can always take
\mathcal{Z}
to be an analytic space, see below), we call
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathcal{Z})
a correspondence space. At the moment the subscripts
DR
and
B
are merely suggestive, and the abstract definition is symmetric in the two “realizations”. We will be mainly interested in correspondence stacks where
\mathcal{Z}
is the graph of a holomorphic map
\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\mathrm{an}}\to\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\mathrm{an}}
. We often refer to the correspondence stack as just the pair
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})
when the correspondence
\mathcal{Z}
is clear.

A morphism of correspondence stacks
f:(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime})
consists of algebraic morphisms
f_{DR}:\mathcal{M}_{DR}\to\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime}
and
f_{B}:\mathcal{M}_{B}\to\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime}
such that the image of the natural map
\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime\mathrm{an}}\times\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime\mathrm{an}}
is contained in the image of
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
on the level of points—that is, if the natural map
\mathcal{Z}\times_{(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime\mathrm{an}}\times\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime\mathrm{an}})}\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Z}
is surjective on points. Observe that with this notion of morphism, any correspondence stack
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathcal{Z})
is naturally isomorphic to the correspondence stack
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B},\tilde{\mathcal{Z}})
obtained by replacing the analytic correspondence by
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}
for any morphism
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}\to\mathcal{Z}
of finite type analytic stacks which is surjective on points. A morphism
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime})
is then given by compatible morphisms
f_{DR}:\mathcal{M}_{DR}\to\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime}
,
f_{B}:\mathcal{M}_{B}\to\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime}
,
\phi:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
up to replacing
\mathcal{Z}
in this way.

For every algebraic stack
\mathcal{M}
defined over
\mathbb{C}
and every field automorphism
\sigma\in\Aut(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q})
, we write
\mathcal{M}^{\sigma}:=\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\sigma}\mathbb{C}
. For a subfield
K\subset\mathbb{C}
, a
K
-correspondence stack
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\{\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}\})
consists of an algebraic stack
\mathcal{M}_{DR}
defined over
\mathbb{C}
and correspondence stacks
(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\sigma},\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma},\mathcal{Z}_{\sigma})
for each
\sigma\in\Aut(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q})
, where each
\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}
is defined over
K
.
A morphism of
K
-correspondence stacks
f:(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\{\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}\})\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\{\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}^{\prime}\})
consists of a morphism
f:\mathcal{M}_{DR}\to\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime}
and
K
-morphisms
f_{B,\sigma}:\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}\to\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}^{\prime}
such that
(f^{\sigma},f_{B,\sigma}):(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\sigma},\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma})\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime\sigma},\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}^{\prime})
is a morphism of correspondence stacks for each
\sigma
. Given a correspondence stack
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})
with
\mathcal{M}_{DR}
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathcal{M}_{B}
defined over
K
, we get a natural
K
-correspondence stack
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\{\mathcal{M}_{B}\})
by taking all the correspondences to be the same as that of
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})
.

Example 5.6
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}
-module. There is a natural correspondence space
\operatorname{Exp}(\Lambda):=(\mathbf{G}_{a}\otimes\Lambda,\mathbf{G}_{m}\otimes\Lambda)
whose correspondence is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\otimes\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\otimes\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{*}\otimes\Lambda.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp\otimes 1}

As above, there is a naturally associated
\mathbb{Q}
-correspondence space
\operatorname{Exp}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Lambda):=(\mathbf{G}_{a}\otimes\Lambda,\{\mathbf{G}_{m}\otimes\Lambda\})
. The construction is functorial in that a homomorphism
\Lambda\to\Lambda^{\prime}
yields a morphism
(\mathbf{G}_{a}\otimes\Lambda,\mathbf{G}_{m}\otimes\Lambda)\to(\mathbf{G}_{a}\otimes\Lambda^{\prime},\mathbf{G}_{m}\otimes\Lambda^{\prime})
of correspondence spaces, as well as a morphism
(\mathbf{G}_{a}\otimes\Lambda,\{\mathbf{G}_{m}\otimes\Lambda\})\to(\mathbf{G}_{a}\otimes\Lambda^{\prime},\{\mathbf{G}_{m}\otimes\Lambda^{\prime}\})
of
\mathbb{Q}
-correspondence spaces.

Taking
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, denote
\operatorname{Exp}(n):=\operatorname{Exp}(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
. Taking the quotient of both sides by the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we have a natural correspondence space
\Sym\operatorname{Exp}(n):=(\Sym^{n}\mathbf{G}_{a},\Sym^{n}\mathbf{G}_{m})
as well as its powers. There is a natural quotient morphism
\operatorname{Exp}(n)\to\Sym\operatorname{Exp}(n)
. Likewise for the
K
-analogs,
\operatorname{Exp}_{K}(\Lambda),\operatorname{Exp}_{K}(n)
and
\Sym\operatorname{Exp}_{K}(n)
.

5.3.
Bialgebraic subsets

Let
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})
be a correspondence stack with correspondence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\mathrm{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\mathrm{an}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{DR}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}}

Definition 5.7
.

(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})
is
irreducible bialgebraic
if
\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B},\mathcal{Z}
are irreducible and
\operatorname{img}\pi_{DR}
and
\operatorname{img}\pi_{B}
have nonempty interior. We say a pair
(\Sigma_{DR},\Sigma_{B})\subset(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})
of constructible subsets
\Sigma_{DR}\subset\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\mathbb{C})
and
\Sigma_{B}\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(\mathbb{C})
is
bialgebraic
if there is a correspondence stack
(\mathcal{N}_{DR},\mathcal{N}_{B})
which is a finite disjoint union of irreducible bialgebraic correspondence stacks and a morphism
f:(\mathcal{N}_{DR},\mathcal{N}_{B})\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})
such that
f_{DR}(\mathcal{N}_{DR}(\mathbb{C}))^{\mathrm{Zar}}=\Sigma_{DR}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
and
f_{B}(\mathcal{N}_{B}(\mathbb{C}))^{\mathrm{Zar}}=\Sigma_{B}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
. We say a constructible
\Sigma_{DR}\subset\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\mathbb{C})
(resp.
\Sigma_{B}\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(\mathbb{C})
) is
bialgebraic
if it can be completed to a bialgebraic pair
(\Sigma_{DR},\Sigma_{B})
.

By definition, bialgebraicity is a property of the Zariski closures. We immediately have the following:

Lemma 5.8
.

A constructible subset
\Sigma_{DR}\subset\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\mathbb{C})
(resp.
\Sigma_{B}\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(\mathbb{C})
) is bialgebraic if and only if for every irreducible component
\Sigma_{0}
of
\Sigma_{DR}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
(resp.
\Sigma_{B}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
) there is an irreducible component
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
of
\pi_{DR}^{-1}(\Sigma_{0})
(resp.
\pi_{B}^{-1}(\Sigma_{0})
) such that
\pi_{B}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
(resp.
\pi_{DR}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
) has nonempty interior in
\pi_{B}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
(resp.
\pi_{DR}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\mathrm{Zar}}
).

We have the following consequence of
Lemma

5.5
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
f:(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime})
be a morphism of correspondence stacks.

(1)

If
(\Sigma_{DR},\Sigma_{B})\subset(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})
is bialgebraic, then so is
(f_{DR}(\Sigma_{DR}),f_{B}(\Sigma_{B}))\subset(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime})
.

(2)

Assume
f_{DR},f_{B}
are both universally open, and that the natural map

(5.3.1)

\mathcal{Z}\times_{(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime\mathrm{an}}\times\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime\mathrm{an}})}\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\mathrm{an}}\times\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\mathrm{an}})\times_{(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime\mathrm{an}}\times\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime\mathrm{an}})}\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}

is surjective on points. Then if
(\Sigma_{DR}^{\prime},\Sigma_{B}^{\prime})\subset(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime})
is bialgebraic, so is
(f_{DR}^{-1}(\Sigma_{DR}^{\prime}),f_{B}^{-1}(\Sigma_{B}^{\prime}))\subset(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B})
.

Proof.

(1) is immediate from
Lemma

5.5
. For (2), observe that the condition on the correspondence in (
5.3.1
) is preserved under base-change. For
(\mathcal{N}_{DR},\mathcal{N}_{B})
a disjoint union of irreducible bialgebraic correspondence stacks and
g:(\mathcal{N}_{DR},\mathcal{N}_{B})\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime})
a morphism, we may therefore reduce to the base-change

(\mathcal{N}_{DR},\mathcal{N}_{B})=(\mathcal{N}_{DR}^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{B})\to(\mathcal{N}_{DR}^{\prime},\mathcal{N}_{B}^{\prime})

and so assume
(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime})
is irreducible bialgebraic and
(\Sigma_{DR},\Sigma_{B})=(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime}(\mathbb{C}),\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime}(\mathbb{C}))
. By the universal openness, there is an open set
U_{DR}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime}
with
U_{DR}^{\prime}(\mathbb{C})\subset\Sigma^{\prime}_{DR}
such that
f^{-1}_{DR}(U_{DR}^{\prime}(\mathbb{C}))
is dense in
f^{-1}_{DR}(\Sigma_{DR}^{\prime})
and is a disjoint union of irreducible constructible sets each dominating
U_{DR}^{\prime}
; likewise for
\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime}
. Thus, by restricting to a dense open set on the target and connected components on the source, we may assume each of
\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B}
is a disjoint union of irreducible stacks and
f_{DR},f_{B}
are surjective on each component. Finally, the condition on the map implies
f_{DR}^{-1}(\operatorname{img}\pi^{\prime}_{DR})\subset\operatorname{img}\pi_{DR}
and
f_{B}^{-1}(\operatorname{img}\pi^{\prime}_{B})\subset\operatorname{img}\pi_{B}
. By
Lemma

5.5
, for each irreducible component
\mathcal{N}_{DR}
of
\mathcal{M}_{DR}
there is some irreducible component
\mathcal{N}_{B}
of
\mathcal{M}_{B}
and an irreducible component
\mathcal{W}
of the correspondence such that
(\mathcal{N}_{DR},\mathcal{N}_{B},\mathcal{W})
is irreducible bialgebraic and
(\mathcal{N}_{DR},\mathcal{N}_{B})\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{B}^{\prime})
is a morphism (and likewise for irreducible components of
\mathcal{M}_{B}
), so the conclusion follows.
∎

Let
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\{\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}\})
be a
K
-correspondence stack with correspondences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\sigma})^{\mathrm{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{DR,\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B,\sigma}}

Definition 5.10
.

(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\{\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}\})
is
irreducible bialgebraic
if
(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma})
is irreducible bialgebraic for all
\sigma
. We say a pair
(\Sigma_{DR},\{\Sigma_{B,\sigma}\})\subset(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\{\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}\})
consisting of a constructible subset
\Sigma_{DR}\subset\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\mathbb{C})
and
K
-constructible subsets
\Sigma_{B,\sigma}\subset\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}(\mathbb{C})
is
K
-bialgebraic
if there is a
K
-correspondence stack
(\mathcal{N}_{DR},\{\mathcal{N}_{B,\sigma}\})
which is a finite disjoint union of irreducible bialgebraic
K
-correspondence stacks and a morphism
f:(\mathcal{N}_{DR},\{\mathcal{N}_{B,\sigma}\})\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\{\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}\})
such that
f_{DR}(\mathcal{N}_{DR}(\mathbb{C}))^{\mathrm{Zar}}=\Sigma_{DR}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
and
f_{B}(\mathcal{N}_{B,\sigma}(\mathbb{C}))^{\mathrm{Zar}}=\Sigma_{B,\sigma}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
for all
\sigma
.

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
f:(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma})\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\{\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}^{\prime}\})
be a morphism of
K
-correspondence stacks.

(1)

If
(\Sigma_{DR},\{\Sigma_{B,\sigma}\})\subset(\mathcal{M}_{DR},\{\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}\})
is
K
-bialgebraic, then so is
(f_{DR}(\Sigma_{DR}),\{f_{B,\sigma}(\Sigma_{B,\sigma})\})\subset(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime},\{\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}^{\prime}\})
.

(2)

Assume
f_{DR},f_{B,\sigma}
are all universally open, and that the natural map

\mathcal{Z}_{\sigma}\times_{(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime\mathrm{an}}\times\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}^{\prime\mathrm{an}})}\mathcal{Z}_{\sigma}^{\prime}\to(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\mathrm{an}}\times\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})\times_{(\mathcal{M}_{DR}^{\prime\mathrm{an}}\times\mathcal{M}_{B,\sigma}^{\prime\mathrm{an}})}\mathcal{Z}_{\sigma}^{\prime}

is surjective on points for each
\sigma
. Then if
(\Sigma_{DR}^{\prime},\{\Sigma_{B,\sigma}^{\prime}\})
is
K
-bialgebraic, so is
(f_{DR}^{-1}(\Sigma_{DR}),\{f_{B}^{-1}(\Sigma_{B,\sigma})\})
.

Theorem 5.12

(Ax–Schanuel
[
3
]
, Gelfond–Schneider
[
49
]
)
.

Let
\Lambda
be a finitely-generated free
\mathbb{Z}
-module.

(1)

For any bialgebraic pair
(\Sigma_{DR},\Sigma_{B})
of
\operatorname{Exp}(\Lambda)
,
\Sigma_{DR}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is a finite union of
\mathbb{C}
-translates of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear subspaces.

(2)

For any
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-bialgebraic pair
(\Sigma_{DR},\{\Sigma_{B,\sigma}\})
of
\operatorname{Exp}_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Lambda)
,
\Sigma_{DR}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is a finite union of
\mathbb{Q}
-translates of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear subspaces.

Proof.

Ax–Schanuel implies (1). For (2) we are reduced to the statement for a point by taking closures, components, and quotients, using (1). That point must be defined over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
on the Betti side by definition, as well as on the De Rham side, or else its
\Aut(\mathbb{C}/\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
orbit would be uncountable. Then this is the Gelfond–Schneider theorem.
∎

Corollary 5.13
.

For any
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-bialgebraic pair
(\Sigma_{DR},\{\Sigma_{B,\sigma}\})
of
\Sym\operatorname{Exp}_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}(n)
,
\Sigma_{DR}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is the image of a finite union of
\mathbb{Q}
-translates of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear subspaces under the natural quotient map

\operatorname{Exp}_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}(n)\to\Sym\operatorname{Exp}_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}(n).

In particular, the
\Sigma_{B,\sigma}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
are all the same, and images of
\mathbb{Q}
-points (resp. torsion points) under the quotient map are Zariski dense in
\Sigma_{DR}
(resp. each
\Sigma_{B,\sigma}
).

Proof.

The quotient map is flat and satisfies the condition of
Corollary

5.11
2
.
∎

5.4.
The De Rham stack

In the next few sections, it will be useful to introduce the notion of a countably finite type algebraic (or analytic) stack, by which we mean a stack with a countable open cover by finite type algebraic (or analytic) stacks. One easily adapts the notions of constructible subsets (which are constructible on some finite type substack), correspondence stacks, and bialgebraic pairs to this setting. Note by the Baire category theorem, any irreducible bialgebraic pair in the sense of countably finite type stacks is irreducible bialgebraic in the ordinary sense.

There is a natural analytic stack
\mathcal{M}_{DR}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
of analytic flat vector bundles on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
. In fact, by solving the connection, there is a natural biholomorphism

RH_{X^{\mathrm{an}}}:\mathcal{M}_{DR}(X^{\mathrm{an}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)^{\mathrm{an}}.

Unfortunately
\mathcal{M}_{DR}(X^{\mathrm{an}})
does not in general have a well-behaved algebraic structure capturing the flat vector bundle.

Example 5.14
.

Let
X=\mathbf{G}_{m}
in either the algebraic or the analytic category and consider the stack of rank 1 flat vector bundles
(L,\nabla)
on
X
. The underlying line bundle is necessarily
L\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}
, so the natural action by
H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X})
on
\mathcal{M}_{DR}(X)
sending
(L,\nabla)
to
(L,\nabla+\alpha)
is transitive, while the choice of identification with
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a torsor for
H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{X})
. Thus, we can realize
\mathcal{M}_{DR}(X)
as
[H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times})\backslash H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X})]
, where an invertible function
f
acts on a form
\alpha
as
\alpha\mapsto d\log f+\alpha
. In the analytic category, integrating
\alpha
around the unit circle and exponentiating provides a natural coordinate and provides the isomorphism with the analytification of
\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)\cong[\mathbf{G}_{m}\backslash\mathbf{G}_{m}]
, where
\mathbf{G}_{m}
acts trivially. In the algebraic category,
H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times})
consist of powers
q^{n}
up to scaling,
H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})=\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]dq
, and
\lambda q^{n}
acts by adding
n\frac{dq}{q}
so the quotient is not of finite type.

What is missing in the algebraic description of the De Rham stack is the requirement that the singularities be regular, as indeed Deligne’s version of the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence is an equivalence of categories between the category of local systems on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
and the category of algebraic flat vector bundles on
X
with regular singularities.
This condition makes reference to the existence of a logarithmic extension to a log smooth compactification.

Let us now introduce some terminology. Let
(\bar{X},D)/k
be a proper log smooth algebraic space with
X=\bar{X}\setminus D
. For an algebraic space
T
, let
\mathcal{D}_{\bar{X}_{T}}
be the sheaf of algebraic
\pi_{T}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{T}
-linear differential operators on
\bar{X}_{T}:=\bar{X}\times T
where
\pi_{T}:\bar{X}_{T}\to T
is the projection and let
\mathcal{D}_{\bar{X}_{T}/T}[\log D]\subset\mathcal{D}_{\bar{X}_{T}/T}
be the
\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}_{T}}
-subalgebra generated by germs of
\pi_{T}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{T}
-linear derivations of
\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}_{T}}
which preserve
\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}_{T}}(-D_{T})
. A family of rank
r
logarithmic connections on
(\bar{X},D)
parametrized by an algebraic space
T
is a
\mathcal{D}_{\bar{X}_{T}/T}[\log D]
-module
E
on
\bar{X}_{T}
which is locally free of rank
r
as a
\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}_{T}}
-module. Given a point
x\in X(\mathbb{C})
, a family of framed (at
x
) rank
r
logarithmic connections on
(\bar{X},D)
parametrized by a scheme
T
is a family of logarithmic connections
E
together with a trivialization
E|_{x\times T}\cong\mathcal{O}_{T}^{r}
as
\mathcal{O}_{T}
-modules.

Theorem 5.15

(
[
94
, Proposition 3.3]
+\epsilon
)
.

Let
T
be an algebraic space and let
\mathcal{E}
be a coherent sheaf on
\bar{X}_{T}
which is flat over
T
. Consider the functor on
T
-spaces which associates to

f:T^{\prime}\rightarrow T
the set of all
\mathcal{D}_{\bar{X}_{T^{\prime}}/T^{\prime}}[\log D]
-module structures on
(1_{X}\times f)^{\ast}\mathcal{E}
. Then this functor is represented by a an algebraic space of finite type over
T
.

Proof.

The statement of
[
94
]
assumes
(\bar{X},D)
is projective. We may take a log-smooth projective modification
\pi:(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to(\bar{X},D)
and apply the theorem to
\pi^{*}E
. To check that a connection
\nabla
descends, it suffices to check for a fixed meromorphic basis
s_{i}
of
E
that the sections
\nabla s_{i}
are pulled back from
\bar{X}
.
∎

The stack of locally free sheaves on
\bar{X}
is countably finite type, and it follows that the stack
\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X},D)
of logarithmic connections is countably finite type. The analytification
\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X},D)^{\mathrm{an}}
is naturally identified with the analytic stack
\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\mathrm{an}},D^{\mathrm{an}})
of analytic logarithmic connections by GAGA.

Example 5.16
.

Continuing
Example

5.14
, let
X=\mathbf{G}_{m}
again and take
\bar{X}=\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The De Rham stack
\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X},D,1)
of rank one logarithmic connections is a union over
k\in\mathbb{Z}
of
[\mathbf{G}_{m}\backslash H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D))]
. On the
k
th factor we associate to a log one-form
\alpha
the logarithmic connection
(\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}}(k),d+\alpha)
where we consider
\mathcal{O}_{\bar{X}}(k)
as the sheaf of functions with at worst a
k
-order pole at
\infty
and
\mathbf{G}_{m}
acts trivially.

5.5.
The Riemann–Hilbert correspondence stack

See
Section

4.1
for the basic properties of the Betti stack. Let
(\bar{X},D)
be a proper log smooth algebraic space with
X=\bar{X}\setminus D
. We define the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence stack
\mathcal{M}_{RH}(\bar{X},D)
of
(\bar{X},D)
to be the correspondence stack
(\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X},D),\mathcal{M}_{B}(X))
with correspondences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\mathrm{an}},D^{\mathrm{an}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{DR}(X^{\mathrm{an}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X},D)^{\mathrm{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)^{\mathrm{an}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RH_{X^{\mathrm{an}}}}

where the unlabelled arrow is restriction to
X
. We denote the resulting analytic morphism
RH_{(\bar{X},D)}:\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X},D)^{\mathrm{an}}\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)^{\mathrm{an}}
. We define the Riemann–Hilbert
\mathbb{Q}
-correspondence stack
\mathcal{M}_{RH,\mathbb{Q}}(\bar{X},D)
to be the
\mathbb{Q}
-correspondence stack
(\mathcal{M}_{DR}(X),\{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X^{\sigma})\})
whose correspondences are given by the graph of
RH_{(\bar{X}^{\sigma},D^{\sigma})}
.

Theorem 5.17

(Simpson
[
106
, Theorem 3.1(c)]
,
[
106
, Theorem 6.1]
)
.

Let
A
be a complex abelian variety.

(1)

For any bialgebraic pair
(\Sigma_{DR},\Sigma_{B})
of
\mathcal{M}_{RH}(A,1)
,
\Sigma_{B}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is a finite union of translates of pullbacks of
\mathcal{M}_{B}(A^{\prime},1)
for abelian variety quotients
A\to A^{\prime}
.

(2)

For any
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-bialgebraic pair
(\Sigma_{DR},\Sigma_{B})
of
\mathcal{M}_{RH,\mathbb{Q}}(A,1)
,
\Sigma_{B}^{\mathrm{Zar}}
is a finite union of torsion translates of pullbacks of
\mathcal{M}_{B}(A^{\prime},1)
for abelian variety quotients
A\to A^{\prime}
.

Note that by the Remark in
[
106
, §6]
, the hypotheses of
Theorem

5.17
agree with those of
[
112
, Theorem 6.1]
, except the latter requires algebraicity in the Dolbeault realization but doesn’t need it.

Remark 5.18
.

If
\mathbb{D}\subset\mathbb{C}
is the unit disk centered at the origin, we can interpret
\Sym\operatorname{Exp}_{\mathbb{Q}}(n)
as the coarse Riemann–Hilbert stack
M_{RH,\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{D},0,n)
, the rank
n
substack of
M_{RH,\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{D},0)
.

5.6.
The residue map

Let
(\bar{X},D)
be a proper log-smooth algebraic space with
X=\bar{X}\setminus D
and denote by
\{D_{i}\}_{i\in I}
the irreducible components of the boundary
D
. Fix a
k
-point
x\in X(k)
. For each
i
we have a conjugacy class of loops
[\gamma_{i}]
around
D_{i}
and a representation
\rho\in R_{B}(X,x,r)(\mathbb{C})
is said to have quasiunipotent local monodromy if
\rho(\gamma_{i})
is quasiunipotent for each
i
—that is, the eigenvalues of
\rho(\gamma_{i})
are roots of unity for each
i
.

On the Betti side, for any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{an}},x)
, we get a morphism
\mathrm{Char}_{B}(\gamma):R_{B}(X,x,r)\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}_{\mathbb{Z}}
defined over
\mathbb{Z}
, given by the composition of the evaluation map and taking the characteristic polynomial. Clearly, this map depends only on the conjugation class of
\gamma
. Since every
D_{i}
provides a conjugacy class of simple loops around it, we obtain a morphism
\mathrm{Char}_{B}(\bar{X},D,x):R_{B}(X,x,r)\rightarrow(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{\mathbb{Z}})^{I}
, which clearly descends to a
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-morphism

\mathrm{Char}_{B}(\bar{X},D,r):\mathcal{M}_{B}(X,r)\to(\Sym^{r}\mathbf{G}_{a})^{I}

where we have identified
\operatorname{img}\mathrm{Char}_{B}(\gamma)\cong\Sym^{r}\mathbf{G}_{a}
over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
by taking roots of the associated polynomial. We say a local system
V\in\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)(\mathbb{C})
has quasiunipotent local monodromy if some (hence any) framing has quasiunipotent local monodromy, or equivalently if
\mathrm{Char}_{B}(\bar{X},D)(V)
is the image of a torsion point of some
((\mathbf{G}_{m})^{r})^{I}
.

On the De Rham side, suppose we have a family of framed rank
n
log-connections on
(\bar{X},D)
parametrized by a
k
-scheme
T
. Then we obtain a
k
-morphism
\mathrm{Char}_{DR}(\infty):T\to(\mathbb{A}^{r}_{k})^{I}
by taking the characteristic polynomials of the residues along the boundary components. In particular, we obtain a
k
-morphism

\mathrm{Char}_{DR}(\bar{X},D,r):\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X},D,r)\to(\Sym^{r}\mathbf{G}_{a})^{I}.

We say a point
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X},D)(\mathbb{C})
has quasiunipotent local monodromy if
\mathrm{Char}_{DR}(\bar{X},D)(\mathcal{V})
is the image of a
\mathbb{Q}
-point of
((\mathbf{G}_{a})^{r})^{I}
.

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X},D)(\mathbb{C})
. The following are equivalent.

(1)

\mathcal{V}
has quasiunipotent local monodromy;

(2)

RH_{X^{\mathrm{an}}}(\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{an}})
has quasiunipotent local monodromy;

(3)

RH_{(X^{\sigma})^{\mathrm{an}}}((\mathcal{V}^{\sigma})^{\mathrm{an}})
has quasiunipotent local monodromy for every
\sigma\in\Aut(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q})
.

Lemma 5.20
.

There is a morphism

\mathrm{Char}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\bar{X},D,r):\mathcal{M}_{RH,\mathbb{Q}}(\bar{X},D,r)\to\Sym\operatorname{Exp}_{\mathbb{Q}}(r)^{I}

of
\mathbb{Q}
-correspondence stacks whose underlying maps on algebraic stacks are

(\mathrm{Char}_{DR}(\bar{X},D,r),\{\mathrm{Char}_{B}(\bar{X}^{\sigma},D^{\sigma},r)\}).

Proof.

The residue is an algebraic map, and therefore compatible with the action of
\Aut(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q})
. In a polydisk neighborhood of the boundary, the eigenvalues of the local monodromy are easily seen to be the exponential of the residues, and the characteristic polynomial is independent of the basepoint.
∎

5.7.
Normal algebraic spaces

We now make sense of the Riemann–Hilbert stack for a normal complex algebraic space
X
. On the Betti side this is straightforward: setting
i:X^{\mathrm{reg}}\to X
to be the regular locus, since
\pi_{1}((X^{\mathrm{reg}})^{\mathrm{an}},x)\to\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{an}},x))
is surjective for any basepoint
x\in X^{\mathrm{reg}}
, we have a closed immersion
i^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X^{\mathrm{reg}})
.

Let
\pi:X^{\prime}\to X
be a log resolution which is an isomorphism over
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
and let
E
be the exceptional locus. Then there is a lift
i^{\prime}:X^{\mathrm{reg}}\to X^{\prime}
and for the same reason
\pi^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X^{\prime})
and
i^{\prime*}:\mathcal{M}_{B}(X^{\prime})\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X^{\mathrm{reg}})
are closed immersions. We may further assume there is a log smooth compactification
(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})
such that
(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime}:=D^{\prime}\cup\bar{E})
is a log smooth compactification of
X^{\mathrm{reg}}
.

Lemma 5.21
.

Every irreducible component of the reduced preimage
RH_{(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})}^{-1}((i^{*}\mathcal{M}_{B}(X))^{\mathrm{an}})
is algebraic.

Proof.

The preimage is an analytic substack with countably many irreducible components. There is a natural closed immersion

b:\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})

realizing
\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})
as the substack of log connections with vanishing residue along the irreducible components of
E
. Since there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})^{\mathrm{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B}(X^{\prime})^{\mathrm{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})^{\mathrm{an}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{B}(X^{\mathrm{reg}})^{\mathrm{an}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RH_{(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i^{\prime*})^{\mathrm{an}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RH_{(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})}}

It therefore suffices to show every irreducible component of
RH_{(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})}^{-1}((\pi^{*}\mathcal{M}_{B}(X))^{\mathrm{an}})
is algebraic.

For any fiber
F
of
\pi:X^{\prime}\to X
, let
q_{F}:F^{\prime}\to F
be a resolution and consider the induced map

q_{F}^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to\mathcal{M}_{DR}(F^{\prime}).

Then on the level of points,
RH_{(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})}^{-1}((\pi^{*}\mathcal{M}_{B}(X))^{\mathrm{an}})
is the intersection of
((q^{*}_{F})^{\mathrm{an}})^{-1}(RH_{F}^{-1}\mathrm{triv}_{F})
over all
F
, where
\mathrm{triv}_{F}\subset\mathcal{M}_{B}(F)
is the set of trivial local systems on
F
. Since
RH_{F}^{-1}(\mathrm{triv}_{F})
is a closed analytic subset and a countable union of algebraic subsets (namely, those flat bundles admitting a flat meromorphic frame), it follows that each irreducible component of
RH_{F}^{-1}(\mathrm{triv}_{F})
is Zariski closed, and therefore the same is true of
((q^{*}_{F})^{\mathrm{an}})^{-1}(RH_{F}^{-1}\mathrm{triv}_{F})
. By noetherianity, each irreducible component of the intersection is therefore algebraic.
∎

Definition 5.22
.

Given
\pi:X^{\prime}\to X
and
(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})
as above, we define
\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E)
to be the reduced preimage
RH_{(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})}^{-1}((i^{*}\mathcal{M}_{B}(X))^{\mathrm{an}})
with its natural structure as a substack, and we define
RH_{(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E)}:\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E)^{\mathrm{an}}\to\mathcal{M}_{B}(X)^{\mathrm{an}}
to be the restriction of
RH_{(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})}
. We define
(\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E),\mathcal{M}_{B}(X),RH_{(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E)})
as the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence stack
\mathcal{M}_{RH}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E)
.

Both
\pi
and
(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime})
can be chosen to be defined over the field of definition of
X
, and we define
(\mathcal{M}_{DR}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E),\{\mathcal{M}_{B}(X^{\sigma})\},\{RH_{(\bar{X}^{\prime\sigma},D^{\prime\sigma},E^{\sigma})}\})
to be the Riemann–Hilbert
\mathbb{Q}
-correspondence stack
\mathcal{M}_{RH,\mathbb{Q}}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E)
.

Note that by definition we have morphisms of (
\mathbb{Q}
-)correspondence stacks

i^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{RH}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E)\to\mathcal{M}_{RH}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})

i^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{RH,\mathbb{Q}}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E)\to\mathcal{M}_{RH,\mathbb{Q}}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})

which are injective on points in the De Rham realization and closed immersions in all of the Betti realizations.

5.8.
Proof of
Theorem

1.11

We now state a more precise version of
Theorem

1.11
:

Theorem 5.23
.

Let
X
be a connected normal algebraic space and let
(\Sigma_{DR},\{\Sigma_{B,\sigma}\})
be a bialgebraic constructible pair of the Riemann–Hilbert
\mathbb{Q}
-correspondence stack
\mathcal{M}_{RH,\mathbb{Q}}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D^{\prime},E)
of a log smooth compactification of a log resolution. Then the local systems with quasiunipotent local monodromy are Zariski dense in
\Sigma_{DR}
and each
\Sigma_{B,\sigma}
.

Proof.

The restriction to the regular locus is a bialgebraic pair so by
Corollary

5.11
we may assume
X
is smooth. We may further assume
(\Sigma_{DR},\{\Sigma_{B,\sigma}\})
is the image of an irreducible bialgebraic correspondence stack. Let
(\Sigma_{DR}^{\prime},\{\Sigma_{B,\sigma}^{\prime}\})
be the image in
\Sym\operatorname{Exp}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{I}
under the residue map. According to
Corollary

5.11
and
Corollary

5.13
, image of rational points (resp. torsion points) under the quotient map are Zariski dense in
\Sigma_{DR}^{\prime}
(resp. each
\Sigma_{B,\sigma}^{\prime}
), and it follows that the preimages of such points are Zariski dense in
\Sigma_{DR}
(resp. each
\Sigma_{B,\sigma}
), which are exactly the points with quasiunipotent local monodromy.
∎

In the
Corollary

9.16
we will show that the notion of
K
-bialgebraic constructible subsets of the Riemann–Hilbert stacks is equivalent to the notion of absolute
K
-constructible subsets (i.e.
K
-constructible in every Betti realization, with no condition on the De Rham realization) for countable
K\subset\mathbb{C}
.

6.
Harmonic maps towards NPC spaces

We will need to use pluriharmonic bundles in both the archimedean and non-archimedean setting. Recall this means equipping a local system
V
on an algebraic variety
X
with a pluriharmonic metric in the former case and a pluriharmonic norm in the latter case. Either way, the pluriharmonic structure is encoded in an equivariant map
\tilde{X}^{V}\to\Delta
for an appropriate classifying space
\Delta
—a symmetric space in the former case, and a Euclidean building in the latter.

The main goal of this section is to formulate the version of the existence theorem for harmonic maps we will need (
Theorem

6.24
). This follows from known results but does not appear in this form in the literature, so we first give the necessary background from
[
71
]
. We also state some related results that will be needed in subsequent sections.

6.1.
Sobolev spaces

Let
(\Omega,g)
be a Riemannian domain, i.e. a connected open subset of a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
, having the property that its metric completion
\bar{\Omega}
is a compact subset of
M
. Let
(\Delta,d)
be a complete metric space. The space
L^{2}(\Omega,\Delta)
is by definition
the set of Borel-measurable maps
u\colon\Omega\to\Delta
having separable range for which

\int_{\Omega}d^{2}(u(x),Q)\dvol_{g}(x)<\infty

for some
Q\in\Delta
. The space
L^{2}(\Omega,\Delta)
equipped with the distance function
D
given by

D(u,v)=\left(\int_{\Omega}\left(d(u(x),v(x)\right)^{2}\dvol_{g}(x)\right)^{1/2}

is a complete metric space.

For every
\epsilon>0
, let
\Omega_{\epsilon}=\{x\in\Omega\,|\,\text{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)>\epsilon\}
, let
B_{\epsilon}(x)
be the geodesic ball centered at
x\in M
and
S_{\epsilon}(x)=\partial B_{\epsilon}(x)
. Let
d\sigma_{S_{\epsilon}(x)}
be the
(\dim M-1)
-dimensional surface measure on
S_{\epsilon}(x)
. For every
u\in L^{2}(\Omega,\Delta)
and every
\epsilon>0
, let
e^{u}_{\epsilon}\in L^{1}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
be the function defined by

e^{u}_{\epsilon}(x):=\int_{y\in S_{\epsilon}(x)}\frac{d^{2}(u(x),u(y))}{\epsilon^{2}}\frac{d\sigma_{S_{\epsilon}(x)}(y)}{\epsilon^{n-1}}

for
x\in\Omega_{\epsilon}
and zero otherwise.

For
\epsilon>0
and
f\in C_{c}(\Omega)
(the set of continuous real-valued functions with compact support), let

E_{\epsilon}^{u}(f):=\int_{\Omega}f\cdot e^{u}_{\epsilon}(x)\dvol_{g}(x).

We say that
u\in L^{2}(\Omega,\Delta)
has finite energy, and we write
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
, if

E(u):=\sup_{f\in C_{c}(\Omega),0\leq f\leq 1}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}E_{\epsilon}^{u}(f)<\infty.

In particular,
W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
contains all Lipschitz continuous maps from
\Omega
to
\Delta
. If
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
, then the measures
e^{u}_{\epsilon}(x)\dvol_{g}(x)
converge weakly to a limiting measure which is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, and hence may
be written as
e^{u}(x)\dvol_{g}(x)
for a function
e^{u}\in L^{1}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
called the energy density of
u
.

Theorem 6.1

(
[
71
, Theorem 1.6.1]
)
.

Let
(u_{k})
be a sequence of maps in
W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
that converges in
L^{2}(\Omega,\Delta)
to a map
u
. If
\sup_{k}E(u_{k})<\infty
, then
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
and
E(u)\leq\liminf_{k}E(u_{k})
.

Remark 6.2
.

If there is an isometric embedding
i\colon(\Delta,d)\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{N},d_{E})
in an Euclidean space, then
W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)=\{u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{N})\,|\,u(x)\in X\text{ a.e. }x\in\Omega\}
and

E(u)=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla(i\circ u)|^{2}.

These definitions are the working definitions adopted in
[
58
]
, where harmonic maps towards locally compact Euclidean buildings were first studied, by locally embedding isometrically Euclidean buildings in Euclidean spaces. In particular, this shows that Korevaar-Schoen definition of the Sobolev space specializes to the classical definition when
(\Delta,d)
is the real line equipped with the Euclidean metric.

When the boundary
\partial\Omega
of
\Omega
in
M
is locally Lipschitz, there is a well-defined trace map
W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)\to L^{2}(\partial\Omega,\Delta)
.

Theorem 6.3

(
[
71
, Corollary 1.6.3 and Theorem 1.12.2]
)
.

Let
(\Omega,g)
be a Lipschitz Riemannian domain and let
(\Delta,d)
be a complete metric space.

(1)

If
u,v\in W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
, then the function
d_{\Delta}(u,v)
is in
W^{1,2}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
, and the condition
tr(u)=tr(v)
is equivalent to
tr(d(u,v))=0
in
L^{2}(\partial\Omega,\mathbb{R})
.

(2)

If the sequence
(u_{i})
of maps in
W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
has uniformly bounded energies
E(u_{i})
, and if
(u_{i})
converges in
L^{2}(\Omega,\Delta)
to a map
u
, then
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
and the trace functions of the
u_{i}
converge in
L^{2}(\partial\Omega,\Delta)
to the trace of
u
.

6.2.
NPC spaces

Definition 6.4
.

A complete metric space
(\Delta,d)
is said to be non-positively curved (NPC), or CAT(0) in Gromov terminology, if for any pair of points
x,y\in\Delta
, there exists a point
m\in\Delta
such that

d(z,m)^{2}+\frac{d(x,y)^{2}}{4}\leq\frac{d(z,x)^{2}+d(z,y)^{2}}{2}

for any
z\in\Delta
.

The point
m
satisfies
d(x,m)=d(y,m)=\frac{d(x,y)}{2}
, and it is uniquely determined by this property. It is called the midpoint of x and y.

Crucially, the energy on
W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
is convex When
(\Delta,d)
is NPC.

6.3.
Harmonic maps

When the boundary
\partial\Omega
of the source
\Omega
in
M
is locally Lipschitz and the target
(\Delta,d)
is a NPC complete metric space, the Dirichlet problem can be solved as follows.

Theorem 6.5

(Korevaar-Schoen
[
71
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

Let
(\Omega,g)
be a Lipschitz Riemannian domain and let
(\Delta,d)
be a NPC complete metric space. Let
\phi\in W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
. Then there exists a unique
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
which minimizes the energy among all maps in
W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
with the same trace as
\phi
. The map
u
is locally Lipschitz continuous in the interior of
\Omega
.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold and
(\Delta,d)
be a complete metric space. A map
u\colon M\to\Delta
is called harmonic if it is a local energy minimizer: every point in
M
admits an open neighborhood with Lipschitz boundary such that all maps in
W^{1,2}(\Omega,\Delta)
which agree with
u
outside of this neighborhood have no less energy.

Thanks to Theorem
6.5
, a harmonic map is locally Lipschitz continuous. In particular, to check that a locally Lipschitz continuous map
u\colon M\to\Delta
is harmonic, it is sufficient to check that every point in
M
admits an open neighborhood with Lipschitz boundary such that every locally Lipschitz continuous map
v\colon M\to\Delta
which agrees with
u
outside of this neighborhood has no less energy.

It will be important to know that the local Lipschitz constant of a harmonic map is controlled by the energy as follows.

Theorem 6.7

(Zhang-Zhong-Zhu, see
[
122
, Theorem 1.4 and Remark 1.5 ii)]
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain (with smooth boundary) of an
n
-dimensional Riemannian
manifold
(M,g)
with
\mathrm{Ric}_{M}\geq-K
for some
K\geq 0
, and let
(\Delta,d)
be a NPC complete metric space. For every
R>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(n,\sqrt{K}R)
such that for every harmonic map
u\colon\Omega\to\Delta
and every
x\in\Omega
with
B_{2R}(x)\Subset\Omega
, the restriction of
u
to
B_{R}(x)
is Lipschitz continuous and the Lipschitz constant is bounded above by
C\int_{B_{R}(x)}e^{u}(x)\dvol_{g}(x)
.

Recall that a metric space is called proper if all of its closed balls are compact; in particular it is locally compact. By the Hopf-Rinow Theorem, a locally compact NPC metric space is proper if and only if it is complete, see
[
10
, Theorem I.2.4]
. Symmetric spaces of noncompact type and locally compact Euclidean Bruhat-Tits buildings are examples of proper CAT(0) spaces.

Theorem 6.8

(Corlette, Gromov-Schoen, Korevaar-Schoen)
.

Let
M
be a complete Riemannian manifold with a finitely generated fundamental group,
\Delta
be a proper NPC metric space and
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(M)\to\mathrm{Isom}(\Delta)
a group homomorphism. Assume that

•

the action of
\rho
does not have a fixed point on
\partial\Delta
;

•

there exists a finite energy
14
14

14

Since the energy density of a
\rho
-equivariant map
u\colon\tilde{M}\to\Delta
is
\rho
-equivariant, it is well-defined on
M
; the map
u
is said to have finite energy if the integral on
M
of its energy density is finite.

\rho
-equivariant map
\tilde{M}\to\Delta
